```text
1	\section{Experiments}
2	
3	We evaluate across two research questions:
4	(1)~Why do complete traces degrade SFT verdict accuracy despite containing all diagnostic information?
5	(2)~Which trace components carry the verdict signal, and how does their density affect learning?
6	All experiments use $N{=}1{,}190$ test samples; accuracy is reported with Wilson 95\% CIs.\footnote{Problem-level partition (472 train / 119 test, zero crossover; \S\ref{sec:dataset}). \texttt{exception\_only} duplicates (48.99\% test; Appendix~\ref{sec:dedup_stats}) arise from within-problem variants; deduplication yields negligible ($<$0.1pp) delta.}
7	Pairwise comparisons use paired bootstrap resampling ($B{=}10{,}000$, seed=42); all differences are significant at $p{<}.0001$ unless noted.
8	Under problem-level clustered bootstrap ($n_\text{eff}{=}119$), all cross-tier gaps remain significant ($p{<}.001$); 3 of 80 within-tier pairwise comparisons lose significance after Holm--Bonferroni correction.
9	
10	\subsection{Cross-Model Comparison}
11	\label{sec:cross_model}
12	
13	Table~\ref{tab:cross_model} compares four models spanning an 8.7$\times$ parameter range (1.5B--13B) and three architecture families under key conditions; single-seed entries are marked with $^\dag$.
14	Regex B5 (99.33\%) exploits the \texttt{[No exception data in trace]} format marker, providing a non-learned baseline; its collapse to 28.49\% under marker removal (\S\ref{sec:nomarker}) shows the SFT advantage is not format-driven.
15	
16	\begin{table}[t]
17	\centering
18	\caption{Cross-model verdict accuracy (\%, $N{=}1{,}190$) across four models (1.5B--13B) and three architecture families. Multi-seed entries report mean$\pm$std over 2--3 seeds; $^\dag$ = single seed (seed 42). Bold: best per model. Full per-seed breakdowns in Appendix~\ref{sec:full_results}; per-class metrics in Appendix~\ref{sec:per_class}.}
19	\label{tab:cross_model}
20	\small
21	\resizebox{\columnwidth}{!}{%
22	\begin{tabular}{lcccc}
23	\toprule
24	\textbf{Condition} & \textbf{Qwen-7B} & \textbf{Qwen-1.5B} & \textbf{CL-13B} & \textbf{DS-6.7B} \\
25	\midrule
26	exception\_only & \textbf{99.83}{\scriptsize$\pm$0.00} & \textbf{99.83}{\scriptsize$\pm$0.00} & \textbf{99.89}{\scriptsize$\pm$0.10} & \textbf{99.39}{\scriptsize$\pm$0.10} \\
27	ae\_only & 99.64{\scriptsize$\pm$0.49} & 98.83{\scriptsize$\pm$0.36} & 99.08{\scriptsize$\pm$0.00} & 98.53{\scriptsize$\pm$0.30} \\
28	VOTA & 99.05{\scriptsize$\pm$0.38} & 99.54{\scriptsize$\pm$0.06} & 98.74{\scriptsize$\pm$0.24} & 99.25$^\ddag$ \\
29	loop\_only & 86.05{\scriptsize$\pm$0.52} & 79.54{\scriptsize$\pm$1.42} & 77.40{\scriptsize$\pm$1.54} & 80.31{\scriptsize$\pm$1.65} \\

45	\paragraph{Exception dominance across families.}
46	Under \texttt{exception\_only}, all four models achieve ${\geq}$99.33\% across all available seeds (Table~\ref{tab:cross_model}), spanning only 0.50pp across architectures (the tightest of any condition).
47	CodeLlama-13B seed 123 achieves 100\%, indicating the 2 persistent errors (\texttt{GeneratorExit}; Appendix~\ref{sec:irreducible}) are convergence artifacts; 99.83\% is a floor, not a ceiling.
48	
49	\paragraph{Scale effects concentrate in Tier~2.}
50	Tier~1 signals are scale-invariant across 1.5B--13B (8.7$\times$): both \texttt{exception\_only} and \texttt{ae\_only} span ${\leq}$1.2pp.
51	\texttt{loop\_only} spans 8.57pp, with CodeLlama-13B below Qwen-1.5B, suggesting architecture matters more than parameter count for lower-density signals.
52	In contrast, \texttt{no\_trace} scales with capacity (Figure~\ref{fig:cross_scale}) and shows the highest seed variance ($\sigma{=}$4.48pp).

58	\label{fig:cross_scale}
59	\end{figure}
60	
61	\paragraph{VOTA cross-family variation.}
62	Excluding outlier seeds, VOTA achieves ${\geq}$98.57\% across all four models (0.80pp range).
63	DeepSeek seed 42 (84.45\%) is a tokenizer-driven truncation failure (91.7\% of samples exceed 2{,}048 tokens after tokenization; Appendix~\ref{sec:tokenizer_analysis}), demonstrating that VOTA's longer sequences are sensitive to tokenizer efficiency: 1/4 models suffers catastrophic truncation under standard training length. \texttt{exception\_only}'s compact inputs (median 150 tokens) sidestep this vulnerability entirely.
64	
65	\subsection{Component Ablation}
66	\label{sec:ablation}
67	
68	Multi-seed \texttt{ae\_only} ablations use Qwen-7B; \texttt{loop\_only} is evaluated across three models to test Tier~2 generalization (Table~\ref{tab:cross_model}).
69	Table~\ref{tab:ablation} decomposes VOTA on Qwen-2.5-7B and reveals a two-tier hierarchy validated across all four models and three architecture families (Table~\ref{tab:cross_model}): Tier~1 (${\geq}$98.53\%) and Tier~2 (77--86\%) are separated by 12+pp with non-overlapping CIs.
70	
71	\begin{table}[t]
72	\centering
73	\caption{Component ablation on Qwen-2.5-7B ($N{=}1{,}190$). Tier~1 signals achieve near-perfect accuracy; combining them (VOTA) slightly dilutes performance. Entries marked $^\dag$/$^\S$/$^\ddag$ are 3-seed mean$\pm$std; unmarked entries are single seed (seed 42). Per-seed breakdowns in Appendix~\ref{sec:full_results}. Seed $\sigma$ negatively correlates with density (0.00 for exception to 0.52 for loop).}
74	\label{tab:ablation}
75	\small
76	\resizebox{\columnwidth}{!}{%
77	\begin{tabular}{llccc}
78	\toprule
79	\textbf{Condition} & \textbf{Components} & \textbf{Acc (\%)} & \textbf{95\% CI} & \textbf{Tier} \\
80	\midrule
81	ae\_only & assertion-point & 99.64$^\dag$ & [99.26, 99.87] & 1 \\
82	exception\_only & exception events & \textbf{99.83} & [99.58, 100.00] & 1 \\
83	VOTA & exc+ae+loop & 99.05$^\S$ & [98.49, 99.50] & --- \\
84	VOTA\_no\_ae & exc+loop & 99.41 & [98.90, 99.80] & --- \\
85	loop\_only & loop compression & 86.05$^\ddag$ & [83.97, 87.96] & 2 \\
86	full\_trace & complete trace & 81.44{\scriptsize$\pm$1.11} & --- & --- \\
87	no\_trace & none & 75.13 & [72.77, 77.48] & --- \\
88	\midrule
89	Regex (best) & exc.\ type regex & 93.61 & --- & --- \\
90	3-shot (Qwen-7B) & in-context learning & 90.84 & --- & --- \\
91	Zero-shot (4 models) & prompting & 51.0--93.5 & --- & --- \\
92	\bottomrule
93	\end{tabular}%
94	}
95	\end{table}
96	
97	Tier~1 signals are individually sufficient: \texttt{exception\_only} (99.83\%$\pm$0.00) exceeds VOTA (99.05\%$\pm$0.38) by +0.78pp (2/3 seeds $p{<}.05$, all directionally consistent), implying that combining signals introduces noise.
98	Removing assertion points has no effect (\texttt{VOTA\_no\_ae} = 99.41\%, single seed), indicating exception dominance.
99	Individual exception subcomponents (type, stacktrace, message) each independently achieve ${\geq}$99.50\%, indicating that the binary presence of an exception event, rather than any specific subcomponent, drives Tier~1 performance (Appendix~\ref{sec:exc_subcomponent}).
100	The Tier~1/2 boundary is unambiguous: the lowest Tier~1 condition (\texttt{ae\_only} on DeepSeek, 98.53\%$\pm$0.30, 2 seeds) exceeds the highest Tier~2 (\texttt{loop\_only} on Qwen-7B, 86.05\%, CI [83.97, 87.96]) by 12.48\,pp with non-overlapping CIs (gap statistic supports $k{=}2$ clusters, silhouette ${\geq}$0.74).
101	Tier~2 \texttt{loop\_only} lifts over \texttt{no\_trace} are model-dependent: Qwen +10.92pp (7B) and +7.19pp (1.5B); DeepSeek +6.95pp; CodeLlama +0.93pp (n.s.).
102	\paragraph{Rule-based baseline comparison.}
103	The strongest non-trivial regex heuristic (B4: exception class name matching; Table~\ref{tab:regex}) achieves 93.61\% accuracy (F1=0.876), trailing SFT \texttt{exception\_only} (99.83\%, F1=0.997) by 6.22pp (McNemar $p{<}.001$).
104	The gap concentrates on caught-exception boundary cases: regex flags all exception events as failures, while SFT learns to distinguish caught exceptions (benign, inside \texttt{try/except}) from uncaught faults, correctly resolving 6/8 boundary samples (Appendix~\ref{sec:regex_baselines}).
105	Across all formats, SFT outperforms the best applicable regex by +3.62--23.86pp (Appendix~\ref{sec:regex_baselines}).
106	
107	\paragraph{Zero-shot and few-shot LLM baselines.}
108	Zero-shot prompting spans 51.0--93.5\% across four models (42.44pp range); three-shot ICL on Qwen-7B (90.84\%) improves only 1.76pp.
109	SFT compresses this range to 0.50pp (99.39--99.89\%), closing a 6--49pp gap with 4,720 samples (Appendix~\ref{sec:zeroshot_cross_model}).
110	\paragraph{Frontier model crossover.}
111	Two frontier models illustrate that SFT's advantage depends on input density: under \texttt{no\_trace}, GPT-4o (84.45\%) and GPT-4.1 (77.31\%) outperform SFT Qwen-7B (75.13\%), but under \texttt{exception\_only} the ranking reverses (SFT 99.83\% vs.\ GPT-4o 87.98\%).
112	Compact diagnostic input unlocks SFT's full advantage over general reasoning capacity.
113	
114	\subsection{Format Marker Ablation}
115	\label{sec:nomarker}
116	
117	The \texttt{exception\_only} format includes a \texttt{[No exception data in trace]} marker in all pass-class samples (regex baseline B5 exploits this for 99.33\% accuracy; Appendix~\ref{sec:regex_baselines}).
118	To quantify the marker's contribution, we retrain with all format markers removed (\texttt{nomarker} condition).
119	
120	Qwen-7B 3-seed mean: 97.65\% $\pm$ 0.96 (seeds 42/123/456: 96.55/98.32/98.07\%), a drop of 2.18pp from \texttt{exception\_only} (99.83\%).
121	Qwen-1.5B: 97.82\%, drop of 2.01pp.
122	The marker contribution (${\sim}$2pp) accounts for only ${\sim}$9\% of the total exception lift over \texttt{no\_trace} (24.70pp on 7B).
123	Regex baselines illustrate this decomposition: B5 (\texttt{[No exception data]}$\to$pass, 99.33\% with markers) collapses to 28.49\% without markers, showing it is entirely format-dependent.
124	The strongest semantic regex B4 (exception class matching, 93.61\%) is marker-invariant; SFT \texttt{nomarker} (97.65\%) exceeds it by 4.04pp, isolating the learned semantic contribution beyond pattern matching.
125	
126	\paragraph{Cross-dataset transfer.}
127	Bidirectional evaluation demonstrates generalization: HumanEval${\to}$MBPP \texttt{exception\_only} achieves 99.41\% ($N{=}4{,}270$) and MBPP${\to}$HumanEval achieves 100.0\% ($N{=}1{,}640$), while \texttt{no\_trace} drops to near-majority baseline in both directions (70.56\% vs.\ 69.02\% majority; 67.99\% vs.\ 61.65\%).
128	The near-zero transfer loss for exception signals, across independently authored problem sets with no problem overlap, shows SFT learns language-level diagnostic patterns, not dataset-specific artifacts.
129	Under marker removal, cross-dataset accuracy drops to 95.5\% ($-$3.9pp), indicating that the majority of cross-dataset transfer relies on learned diagnostic patterns rather than format markers.
130	
131	\subsection{Decomposing the Full-Trace Degradation}
132	\label{sec:density}
133	
134	The 18.07pp gap between \texttt{exception\_only} (99.83\%) and \texttt{full\_trace} (81.76\%) could reflect gradient dilution (diagnostic tokens are ${\sim}$1/500 of full traces, diluting gradient signal during training) or content quality (verbose input buries diagnostic signals at inference).
135	
136	\paragraph{Controlled gradient masking.}
137	To isolate gradient dilution, we mask 99.8\% of gradient updates during \texttt{exception\_only} training, matching the diagnostic token ratio of full traces while preserving compact input.
138	This yields 99.22\% (mean $-$0.61pp from \texttt{exception\_only}, 3 seeds), limiting gradient dilution to ${\leq}$4\% of the 18.07pp gap (Table~\ref{tab:density}).
139	The gradient masking bound attributes the remaining ${\geq}$96\% to content quality (diagnostic signal density at inference), though forward-pass attention during masked training is an unquantified residual.
140	Note that masking affects only the backward pass; the model still attends to all input tokens during the forward pass, so this bounds gradient dilution's contribution but does not rule out attention-based information flow as a residual confound.
141	
142	\begin{table}[t]
143	\centering
144	\caption{Decomposition of the full-trace degradation (Qwen-7B). Gradient masking attributes ${\leq}$4\% to gradient dilution; the bound assigns the remainder to content quality, though forward-pass attention is a residual confound (\S\ref{sec:density}). Label-only loss provides a confounded but directionally consistent complementary estimate.}
145	\label{tab:density}
146	\small
147	\begin{tabular}{lcc}
148	\toprule
149	\textbf{Condition} & \textbf{Acc (\%)} & \textbf{$\Delta$} \\
150	\midrule
151	exception\_only & 99.83{\scriptsize$\pm$0.00} & --- \\
152	\quad + grad mask (99.8\%) & 99.22{\scriptsize$\pm$0.20}$^\S$ & $-$0.61pp \\
153	full\_trace & 81.76$^\ddag$ & $-$18.07pp \\
154	\midrule
155	\multicolumn{3}{l}{\textit{Complementary (confounded):}} \\
156	full\_trace + label-only loss & 85.94{\scriptsize$\pm$1.12} & +4.50pp$^\dag$ \\
157	\bottomrule
158	\end{tabular}
159	\vspace{2pt}
160	\par\raggedright\footnotesize
161	$^\S$Mean $\pm$ std over 3 seeds (42, 123, 456).
162	$^\ddag$Matched-seed baseline; 3-seed mean 81.44\%$\pm$1.11 (Table~\ref{tab:cross_model}).
163	$^\dag$vs.\ \texttt{full\_trace} (81.44\%$\pm$1.11); not significant under clustered bootstrap (95\% CI [$-$0.6, +9.8]pp, $n_\text{eff}{=}119$).
164	\end{table}
165	
166	\paragraph{Complementary evidence.}
167	Label-only loss on full traces (+4.50pp, 3 seeds) yields a confounded upper bound of ${\leq}$26\% gradient contribution, consistent with the masking estimate (${\leq}$4\%) but not significant under clustered bootstrap (95\% CI [$-$0.6, +9.8]pp).
168	Qwen-1.5B shows a similar label-only pattern (17\%/83\%, 3 seeds); CodeLlama and DeepSeek exhibit label-only collapse to majority baseline (28.5\%), limiting this complementary analysis to the Qwen family.
169	The architecture dependence suggests label-only training is sensitive to model capacity or optimization landscape; the gradient masking ablation provides a more robust, architecture-independent decomposition.
170	Cross-architecture validation on DeepSeek-Coder-6.7B confirms this bound: 99.33\% accuracy under 99.8\% masking (${\leq}$0.3\% dilution of the 20.23pp gap), consistent with the Qwen-7B result.
171	Error analysis supports the content quality interpretation: all 217 \texttt{full\_trace} errors occur where tokenizer truncation removes diagnostic markers, manifesting through mechanical truncation rather than semantic confusion.
172	
173	\subsection{Complementary Analyses}
174	\label{sec:additional}
175	
176	\paragraph{Trace length ablation.}
177	\label{sec:trace_length}
178	Varying the head-truncated trace token budget (256--no limit) on Qwen-7B yields accuracy in a narrow 77.2\%--81.8\% band with a non-monotonic pattern: 1024-token prefixes (77.23\%) underperform 512-token prefixes (80.42\%, $p{=}.002$), because mid-length prefixes retain enough irrelevant content to mislead the model while discarding diagnostic events near the trace terminus (Appendix~\ref{sec:trace_length_full}).
179	Critically, removing the token budget entirely (no truncation) yields 81.76\%, identical to head-2048, ruling out truncation as the explanation for the full-trace degradation.
180	This non-monotonicity is specific to head-truncation; under tail-truncation, accuracy increases monotonically for two of three models (Table~\ref{tab:tail_cross_model} in Appendix), with only DeepSeek showing a mild architecture-specific reversal at tail-1024$\to$tail-2048 ($-$1.5pp).
181	
182	\paragraph{Truncation direction and budget curve.}
183	Tail-truncation (retaining the last $k$ tokens) dramatically outperforms head-truncation: among the three models with complete data, tail-2048 exceeds head-2048 by 15.5--22.6pp, with the gap widening at smaller scale (Qwen-1.5B: +22.6pp vs.\ Qwen-7B: +15.5pp; Appendix~\ref{sec:trace_length_full}, Table~\ref{tab:head_vs_tail}).
184	All four models' head-2048 accuracies converge into a narrow 75.3--81.8\% band (6.5pp), comparable to the tail-256 band (72.6--78.9\%, 6.3pp), indicating that signal-poor conditions produce a shared performance floor regardless of scale or architecture.
185	
186	A cross-model budget sweep across four models and three architecture families reveals a sharp performance jump localized between 384 and 512 tail tokens.
187	At tail-384, all models remain near baseline (73.6--80.7\%, only +1.0--4.8pp over tail-256); the steep jump occurs at 384$\to$512 (+13.2--22.0pp), crossing from near-baseline to near-ceiling (${\sim}$94--98\%).
188	This localizes the critical threshold to ${\sim}$400--500 tokens, crossing the globals module dump threshold (${\sim}$384 tokens) and exposing structured execution markers (Table~\ref{tab:tail_cross_model}).
189	CodeLlama tail conditions use label-only loss (\S\ref{sec:density}), which may inflate its tail accuracies; cross-model tail comparisons should account for this training difference.
190	Beyond 512 tokens, accuracy converges further: multi-seed validation across all 12 model$\times$seed combinations shows all tail-512 accuracies fall within a 4.63pp band [93.61\%, 98.24\%] (Table~\ref{tab:tail512_seeds}); at tail-1024, three models reach 96.5--98.2\%, compressing the 6.3pp cross-model spread observed at tail-256.
191	\texttt{tail\_2048} captures 95--98\% of \texttt{exception\_only}'s accuracy across all completed configurations.
192	The residual 2--5pp gap indicates that semantic extraction (identifying exception type and message) still outperforms positional extraction (retaining a fixed window containing non-diagnostic lines).
193	
194	\paragraph{Calibration.}
195	\label{sec:calibration}
196	Calibration mirrors the tier hierarchy: \texttt{exception\_only} achieves ECE = 0.0027 vs.\ 0.171 for \texttt{no\_trace}, a 63$\times$ gap (Figure~\ref{fig:calibration}).
197	This calibration advantage generalizes across architectures and scales: all four models (1.5B--13B) achieve ECE ${<}$ 0.005 under \texttt{exception\_only} (Table~\ref{tab:calibration_model_level} in Appendix), indicating well-calibrated predictions rather than overfit confidence.
198	
199	\paragraph{Error analysis.}
200	\texttt{exception\_only}'s 2 errors have zero overlap with all other conditions' error sets (Jaccard = 0.000; Appendix~\ref{sec:error_overlap}); per-type breakdown shows all standard exception types achieve 100\% accuracy, with only \texttt{GeneratorExit} (a benign \texttt{BaseException} subclass) causing failures (Appendix~\ref{sec:exception_type}).
201	
202	\paragraph{Length control.}
203	Padding \texttt{exception\_only} to 2{,}048 tokens, even in a semantically loaded position (after the verdict region), yields identical accuracy (99.83\%), indicating the 18--23pp gap arises from spurious patterns in verbose trace content, not sequence length.
204	
205	\paragraph{Supplementary OOD analysis.}
206	\label{sec:debugbench}
207	On DebugBench \citep{tian2024debugbench} ($N{=}426$), the condition ranking is preserved: \texttt{exception\_only} 100\%, VOTA 99.77\%, \texttt{no\_trace} 96.71\%.
208	However, narrow margins (3.29pp headroom from \texttt{no\_trace}) limit discriminative power; this supplementary analysis preserves the ranking but cannot establish effect sizes comparable to the primary dataset (Appendix~\ref{sec:debugbench_full}).
209	
```

\\texttt{loop\\_only} lifts over \\texttt{no\\_trace} are model-dependent: Qwen +10.92pp (7B) and +7.19pp (1.5B); DeepSeek +6.95pp; CodeLlama +0.93pp (n.s.).\n\\paragraph{Rule-based baseline comparison.}\nThe strongest non-trivial regex heuristic (B4: exception class name matching; Table~\\ref{tab:regex}) achieves 93.61\\% accuracy (F1=0.876), trailing SFT \\texttt{exception\\_only} (99.83\\%, F1=0.997) by 6.22pp (McNemar $p{<}.001$).\nThe gap concentrates on caught-exception boundary cases: regex flags all exception events as failures, while SFT learns to distinguish caught exceptions (benign, inside \\texttt{try/except}) from uncaught faults, correctly resolving 6/8 boundary samples (Appendix~\\ref{sec:regex_baselines}).\nAcross all formats, SFT outperforms the best applicable regex by +3.62--23.86pp (Appendix~\\ref{sec:regex_baselines}).\n\n\\paragraph{Zero-shot and few-shot LLM baselines.}\nZero-shot prompting spans 51.0--93.5\\% across four models (42.44pp range); three-shot ICL on Qwen-7B (90.84\\%) improves only 1.76pp.\nSFT compresses this range to 0.50pp (99.39--99.89\\%), closing a 6--49pp gap with 4,720 samples (Appendix~\\ref{sec:zeroshot_cross_model}).\n\\paragraph{Frontier model crossover.}\nTwo frontier models illustrate that SFT's advantage depends on input density: under \\texttt{no\\_trace}, GPT-4o (84.45\\%) and GPT-4.1 (77.31\\%) outperform SFT Qwen-7B (75.13\\%), but under \\texttt{exception\\_only} the ranking reverses (SFT 99.83\\% vs.\\ GPT-4o 87.98\\%).\nCompact diagnostic input unlocks trace]} marker in all pass-class samples (regex baseline B5 exploits this for 99.33\\% accuracy; Appendix~\\ref{sec:regex_baselines}).\nTo quantify the marker's contribution, we retrain with all baselines illustrate this decomposition: B5 (\\texttt{[No exception data]}$\\to$pass, 99.33\\% with markers) collapses to 28.49\\% without markers, showing it is entirely format-dependent.\nThe strongest semantic regex B4 (exception class matching, 93.61\\%) is marker-invariant; SFT \\texttt{nomarker} (97.65\\%) exceeds it by 4.04pp, isolating the learned semantic contribution beyond pattern matching.\n\n\\paragraph{Cross-dataset transfer.}\nBidirectional evaluation demonstrates generalization: HumanEval${\\to}$MBPP \\texttt{exception\\_only} achieves 99.41\\% ($N{=}4{,}270$) and MBPP${\\to}$HumanEval achieves 100.0\\% ($N{=}1{,}640$), while \\texttt{no\\_trace} drops to near-majority baseline in both directions (70.56\\% vs.\\ 69.02\\% majority; 67.99\\% vs.\\ 61.65\\%).\nThe near-zero transfer loss for exception signals, across independently authored problem sets with no problem overlap, shows SFT learns language-level diagnostic patterns, not dataset-specific artifacts.\nUnder marker removal, cross-dataset accuracy drops to 95.5\\% ($-$3.9pp), indicating that the majority of cross-dataset transfer relies on learned diagnostic patterns rather than format markers.\n\n\\subsection{Decomposing the Full-Trace Degradation}\n\\label{sec:density}\n\nThe 18.07pp gap between \\texttt{exception\\_only} (99.83\\%) and \\texttt{full\\_trace} (81.76\\%) could reflect gradient dilution (diagnostic tokens are ${\\sim}$1/500 of full traces, diluting gradient signal during training) or content quality (verbose input buries diagnostic signals at inference).\n\n\\paragraph{Controlled gradient masking.}\nTo isolate gradient dilution, we mask 99.8\\% of gradient updates during \\texttt{exception\\_only} training, matching the diagnostic token ratio of full traces while preserving compact input.\nThis yields 99.22\\% (mean $-$0.61pp from \\texttt{exception\\_only}, 3 seeds), limiting gradient dilution to ${\\leq}$4\\% of the 18.07pp gap (Table~\\ref{tab:density}).\nThe gradient masking bound attributes the remaining ${\\geq}$96\\% to content quality (diagnostic signal density at inference), though forward-pass attention during masked training is an unquantified residual.\nNote that masking affects only the backward pass; the model still attends to all input tokens during the forward pass, so this bounds gradient dilution's contribution but does not rule out attention-based information flow as a residual confound.\n\n\\begin{table}[t]\n\\centering\n\\caption{Decomposition of the full-trace degradation (Qwen-7B). Gradient masking attributes ${\\leq}$4\\% to gradient dilution; the bound assigns the remainder to content quality, though forward-pass attention is a residual confound (\\S\\ref{sec:density}). Label-only loss provides a confounded but directionally consistent complementary estimate.}\n\\label{tab:density}\n\\small\n\\begin{tabular}{lcc}\n\\toprule\n\\textbf{Condition} & \\textbf{Acc (\\%)} & \\textbf{$\\Delta$} \\\\\n\\midrule\nexception\\_only & 99.83{\\scriptsize$\\pm$0.00} & --- \\\\\n\\quad + grad mask (99.8\\%) & 99.22{\\scriptsize$\\pm$0.20}$^\\S$ & $-$0.61pp \\\\\nfull\\_trace & 81.76$^\\ddag$ & $-$18.07pp \\\\\n\\midrule\n\\multicolumn{3}{l}{\\textit{Complementary (confounded):}} \\\\\nfull\\_trace + label-only loss & 85.94{\\scriptsize$\\pm$1.12} & +4.50pp$^\\dag$ \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\vspace{2pt}\n\\par\\raggedright\\footnotesize\n$^\\S$Mean $\\pm$ std over 3 seeds (42, 123, 456).\n$^\\ddag$Matched-seed baseline; 3-seed mean 81.44\\%$\\pm$1.11 (Table~\\ref{tab:cross_model}).\n$^\\dag$vs.\\ \\texttt{full\\_trace} (81.44\\%$\\pm$1.11); not significant under clustered bootstrap (95\\% CI [$-$0.6, +9.8]pp, $n_\\text{eff}{=}119$).\n\\end{table}\n\n\\paragraph{Complementary evidence.}\nLabel-only loss on full traces (+4.50pp, 3 seeds) yields a confounded upper bound of ${\\leq}$26\\% gradient contribution, consistent with the masking estimate (${\\leq}$4\\%) but not significant under clustered bootstrap (95\\% CI [$-$0.6, +9.8]pp).\nQwen-1.5B shows a similar label-only pattern (17\\%/83\\%, 3 seeds); CodeLlama and DeepSeek exhibit label-only collapse to majority baseline (28.5\\%), limiting this complementary analysis to the Qwen family.\nThe architecture dependence suggests label-only training is sensitive to model capacity or optimization landscape; the gradient masking ablation provides a more robust, architecture-independent decomposition.\nCross-architecture validation on DeepSeek-Coder-6.7B confirms this bound: 99.33\\% accuracy under 99.8\\% masking (${\\leq}$0.3\\% dilution of the 20.23pp gap), consistent with the Qwen-7B result.\nError analysis supports the content quality interpretation: all 217 \\texttt{full\\_trace} errors occur where tokenizer truncation removes diagnostic markers, manifesting through mechanical truncation rather than semantic confusion.\n\n\\subsection{Complementary Analyses}\n\\label{sec:additional}\n\n\\paragraph{Trace length ablation.}\n\\label{sec:trace_length}\nVarying the head-truncated trace token budget (256--no limit) on Qwen-7B yields accuracy in a narrow 77.2\\%--81.8\\% band with a non-monotonic pattern: 1024-token prefixes (77.23\\%) underperform 512-token prefixes (80.42\\%, $p{=}.002$), because mid-length prefixes retain enough irrelevant content to mislead the model while discarding diagnostic events near the trace terminus (Appendix~\\ref{sec:trace_length_full}).\nCritically, removing the token budget entirely (no truncation) yields 81.76\\%, identical to head-2048, ruling out truncation as the explanation for the full-trace degradation.\nThis non-monotonicity is specific to head-truncation; under tail-truncation, accuracy increases monotonically for two of three models (Table~\\ref{tab:tail_cross_model} in Appendix), with only DeepSeek showing a mild architecture-specific reversal at tail-1024$\\to$tail-2048 ($-$1.5pp).\n\n\\paragraph{Truncation direction and budget curve.}\nTail-truncation (retaining the last $k$ tokens) dramatically outperforms head-truncation: among the three models with complete data, tail-2048 exceeds head-2048 by 15.5--22.6pp, with the gap widening at smaller scale (Qwen-1.5B: +22.6pp vs.\\ Qwen-7B: +15.5pp; Appendix~\\ref{sec:trace_length_full}, Table~\\ref{tab:head_vs_tail}).\nAll four models' head-2048 accuracies converge into a narrow 75.3--81.8\\% band (6.5pp), comparable to the tail-256 band (72.6--78.9\\%, 6.3pp), indicating that signal-poor conditions produce a shared performance floor regardless of scale or architecture.\n\nA cross-model budget sweep across four models and three architecture families reveals a sharp performance jump localized between 384 and 512 tail tokens.\nAt tail-384, all models remain near baseline (73.6--80.7\\%, only +1.0--4.8pp over tail-256); the steep jump occurs at 384$\\to$512 (+13.2--22.0pp), crossing from near-baseline to near-ceiling (${\\sim}$94--98\\%).\nThis localizes the critical threshold to ${\\sim}$400--500 tokens, crossing the globals module dump threshold (${\\sim}$384 tokens) and exposing structured execution markers (Table~\\ref{tab:tail_cross_model}).\nCodeLlama tail conditions use label-only loss (\\S\\ref{sec:density}), which may inflate its tail accuracies; cross-model tail comparisons should account for this training difference.\nBeyond 512 tokens, accuracy converges further: multi-seed validation across all 12 model$\\times$seed combinations shows all tail-512 accuracies fall within a 4.63pp band [93.61\\%, 98.24\\%] (Table~\\ref{tab:tail512_seeds}); at tail-1024, three models reach 96.5--98.2\\%, compressing the 6.3pp cross-model spread observed at tail-256.\n\\texttt{tail\\_2048} captures 95--98\\% of \\texttt{exception\\_only}'s accuracy across all completed configurations.\nThe residual 2--5pp gap indicates that semantic extraction (identifying exception type and message) still outperforms positional extraction (retaining a fixed window containing non-diagnostic lines).\n\n\\paragraph{Calibration.}\n\\label{sec:calibration}\nCalibration mirrors the tier hierarchy: \\texttt{exception\\_only} achieves ECE = 0.0027 vs.\\ 0.171 for \\texttt{no\\_trace}, a 63$\\times$ gap (Figure~\\ref{fig:calibration}).\nThis calibration advantage generalizes across architectures and scales: all four models (1.5B--13B) achieve ECE ${<}$ 0.005 under \\texttt{exception\\_only} (Table~\\ref{tab:calibration_model_level} in Appendix), indicating well-calibrated predictions rather than overfit confidence.\n\n\\paragraph{Error analysis.}\n\\texttt{exception\\_only}'s 2 errors have zero overlap with all other conditions' error sets (Jaccard = 0.000; Appendix~\\ref{sec:error_overlap}); per-type breakdown shows all standard exception types achieve 100\\% accuracy, with only \\texttt{GeneratorExit} (a benign \\texttt{BaseException} subclass) causing failures (Appendix~\\ref{sec:exception_type}).\n\n\\paragraph{Length control.}\nPadding \\texttt{exception\\_only} to 2{,}048 tokens, even in a semantically loaded position (after the verdict region), yields identical accuracy (99.83\\%), indicating the 18--23pp gap arises from spurious patterns in verbose trace content, not sequence length.\n\n\\paragraph{Supplementary OOD analysis.}\n\\label{sec:debugbench}\nOn DebugBench \\citep{tian2024debugbench} ($N{=}426$), the condition ranking is preserved: \\texttt{exception\\_only} 100\\%, VOTA 99.77\\%, \\texttt{no\\_trace} 96.71\\%.\nHowever, narrow margins (3.29pp headroom from \\texttt{no\\_trace}) limit discriminative power; this supplementary analysis preserves the ranking but cannot establish effect sizes comparable to the primary dataset (Appendix~\\ref{sec:debugbench_full}).\n",

```text
17	\begin{document}
18	\maketitle
19	
20	\begin{abstract}
21	Complete execution traces contain all information needed for verdict prediction (classifying whether a test passes or fails), yet they actively impair SFT learning.
22	Across four code-specialized models (1.5B--13B, three architecture families), complete traces achieve only 77--82\% while compact exception events reach ${\geq}$99.33\%.
23	A regex exploiting format markers matches this ceiling, but marker removal collapses it to 28.49\% while SFT retains 97.65\%; cross-dataset transfer shows SFT acquires diagnostic patterns beyond format matching.
24	Gradient masking attributes ${\leq}$4\% of the 18--23pp gap to gradient dilution; content quality at inference explains the majority, though forward-pass attention remains an unquantified residual.
25	An empirical two-tier signal hierarchy holds across families, yielding a practical strategy: extract exception events first; when unavailable, tail-truncated traces provide a viable fallback.
26	\end{abstract}
27	
28	\input{introduction}
29	\input{method}
30	\input{experiments}
31	\input{discussion}
32	\input{related_work}
33	\input{conclusion}
34	
35	\section*{Limitations}
36	
37	Our study has several limitations.
38	First, all experiments use HumanEval and MBPP-sanitized (591 problems, simple assert-based tests on short Python functions); generalization to human-written code, other languages, non-assert test frameworks (e.g., parametric tests, integration tests, flaky tests), and multi-file projects is unverified.
39	Second, all four models (1.5B--13B) are code-specialized; models at 30B+ parameters may have sufficient capacity to overcome diagnostic signal dilution, and general-purpose LLMs may behave differently. The gradient masking ablation is validated on two architectures (Qwen-7B, 3 seeds; DeepSeek-6.7B, 1 seed); broader cross-architecture and cross-scale validation remains future work.
40	Third, multi-seed experiments use only 3 seeds; several baselines (\texttt{no\_trace} for 2/4 models, \texttt{full\_trace} for 2/4 models) rely on single-seed results with Wilson CI variance estimates rather than empirical seed distributions.
41	
42	\section*{Ethics Statement}
43	
44	This work uses publicly available benchmarks (HumanEval, MBPP-sanitized, DebugBench) and does not involve human subjects, crowdsourced annotations, or personally identifiable information.
45	All models evaluated are open-source and publicly accessible.
46	Training uses parameter-efficient LoRA fine-tuning on enterprise-grade GPUs, with each experiment using a single GPU.
47	Total compute is approximately 320 GPU-hours across all conditions, models, seeds, and iterative refinements (including approximately 170 hours for successful experiments; individual runs range from 0.5h for small models to 2.2h for CodeLlama-13B, with the total reflecting 4 models $\times$ multiple conditions $\times$ multiple seeds $\times$ iterative development).
48	We do not foresee direct negative societal impacts from this work; verdict prediction is a developer-facing tool for automated testing.
49	
50	\bibliography{references}
51	
52	\appendix
53	\input{appendix}
54	
55	\end{document}
56
```

```text
1	\section{Introduction}
2	
3	Execution traces (line-by-line records of a program's runtime behavior) have emerged as a promising input modality for code language models \citep{chen2025tracepile,ding2024traced,wang2025dcsh,ni2024next}.
4	The intuition is straightforward: more execution information should help the model make better predictions.
5	Yet for verdict prediction (classifying whether a test passes or fails), this intuition is wrong.
6	Complete traces \emph{actively degrade} accuracy compared to compact exception events, despite containing strictly more information.
7	
8	Prior work found trace utility to be task-dependent \citep{wang2025dcsh}, but focused on generation-oriented tasks where output noise confounds the analysis.
9	Verdict prediction offers a cleaner testbed: as binary classification, it isolates the learning signal from generation noise, and controlled component ablation directly measures how trace content affects SFT learning.
10	This approach aligns with recent *ACL precedent: CRUXEval \citep{gu2024cruxeval} uses execution prediction to probe code reasoning, and NExT \citep{ni2024next} uses trace-augmented chain-of-thought for program repair.
11	
12	We present a systematic component ablation of trace representations for verdict prediction.
13	Using 591 problems from HumanEval \citep{chen2021evaluating} and MBPP-sanitized \citep{austin2021program}, we evaluate four models across three architecture families and 1.5B to 13B parameters: Qwen-2.5-Coder-7B/1.5B-Instruct \citep{qwen2024coder}, CodeLlama-13B-Instruct \citep{roziere2024code}, and DeepSeek-Coder-6.7B-Instruct \citep{deepseek2024coder}.
14	
15	The central result: complete traces (77--82\%) barely improve over source code alone, while compact exception events achieve near-perfect accuracy (${\geq}$99\%) across all four models, an 18--23pp gap (\S\ref{sec:cross_model}).
16	A simple regex exploiting a format marker matches this ceiling, so the interesting question is not why exceptions help but \emph{why complete traces hurt}.
17	Marker removal collapses the regex to near-chance while SFT retains $>$97\%; cross-dataset transfer confirms SFT acquires diagnostic patterns beyond format matching (\S\ref{sec:nomarker}).
18	
19	Gradient masking attributes ${\leq}$4\% of the degradation to gradient dilution; content quality at inference accounts for the majority (\S\ref{sec:density}).
20	Training dynamics corroborate this: \texttt{exception\_only} converges within 50 steps while \texttt{full\_trace} oscillates without convergence (Appendix~\ref{sec:training_dynamics}).
21	A tail-truncation budget sweep reveals a sharp performance transition near 512 tokens, coinciding with the first appearance of structured execution markers (\S\ref{sec:trace_length}).
22	
23	While exception events structurally encode verdict direction (exception present typically implies failure), the non-trivial finding is that providing strictly more information (complete traces containing all exception data) degrades performance by 18--23pp.
24	Our contributions:
25	\begin{enumerate}
26	\item \textbf{Complete traces degrade SFT verdict accuracy} despite containing all diagnostic information: across four models (1.5B--13B, three families), complete traces (77--82\%) remain near source-code-only levels while compact exception events close an 18--23pp gap to ${\geq}$99.33\% (\S\ref{sec:cross_model}).
27	
28	\item \textbf{Semantic vs.\ format-marker decomposition}: marker removal collapses a format-exploiting regex by 69pp while SFT drops only 2pp; cross-dataset transfer (99.4--100\%) shows the learned signal generalizes beyond format markers (\S\ref{sec:nomarker}).
29	
30	\item \textbf{A two-tier signal hierarchy}: exception/assertion signals (${\geq}$98.53\%) dominate loop compression (77--86\%) by 12+pp with non-overlapping CIs; a sharp transition at ${\sim}$512 tail tokens yields a practical fallback (\S\ref{sec:ablation}, \S\ref{sec:trace_length}).
31	
32	\item \textbf{Mechanistic decomposition}: controlled gradient masking attributes ${\leq}$4\% of the degradation to gradient dilution, assigning the majority to content quality at inference, though forward-pass attention is an unquantified residual (\S\ref{sec:density}).
33	\end{enumerate}
34	
```

<details><summary>↩ Tool result · `toolu_01M4zJaKGu1oqNvQgTCtp7ph`</summary>

```text
1	\section{Discussion}
2	\label{sec:discussion}
3	
4	\paragraph{A descriptive two-pathway framework.}
5	The results suggest two independent signal pathways and a dilution mechanism that together explain why complete traces degrade SFT learning.
6	
7	\paragraph{Explicit pathway: diagnostic tokens.}
8	The diagnostic token ratio $d$ (\S2.1) correlates strongly with accuracy across five conditions, and all four models achieve near-perfect accuracy under \texttt{exception\_only} (Table~\ref{tab:cross_model}).
9	\texttt{ae\_only}, a strict superset, slightly underperforms (Table~\ref{tab:ablation}), indicating that additional content introduces noise even within exception events.
10	
11	\paragraph{Implicit pathway: terminal execution state.}
12	Tail-truncated traces achieve 93--97\% accuracy (Table~\ref{tab:tail_cross_model}) despite $d {\approx} 0$.
13	At ${\geq}$512 tokens, tail truncation captures \texttt{[Line~X]} variable values at final execution steps, which implicitly encode verdict direction; below 384 tokens, the globals module dump consumes the entire budget, yielding near-\texttt{no\_trace} performance.
14	
15	\paragraph{Diagnostic signal dilution in complete traces.}
16	Both pathways' signals exist in full traces but are buried under thousands of intermediate tokens.
17	Controlled gradient masking (\S\ref{sec:density}) attributes ${\leq}$4\% of the gap to gradient dilution, assigning the majority to content quality at inference: diagnostic tokens are too sparse for the model to reliably extract from verbose input.
18	Training dynamics (Appendix~\ref{sec:training_dynamics}) corroborate this: \texttt{exception\_only} converges within 50 steps while \texttt{full\_trace} oscillates without convergence.
19	
20	\paragraph{Why SFT over regex?}
21	The contribution is the diagnostic decomposition, not a prediction system; nonetheless, SFT dominates regex on every axis beyond raw accuracy.
22	SFT retains 97.65\% under marker removal while the regex collapses to 28.49\% (\S\ref{sec:nomarker}), provides 63$\times$ better calibration (\S\ref{sec:calibration}), and transfers bidirectionally across datasets with negligible loss.
23	
24	\paragraph{Design implications.}
25	Complete traces capture only 4--9pp of the ${\sim}$27pp headroom over source code alone, while exception events capture 24--27pp.
26	This runs counter to the field's operating assumption that more trace information should help \citep{chen2025tracepile,wang2025dcsh}; the magnitude and component decomposition are not predictable from information-theoretic priors alone.
27	The two-tier hierarchy suggests a practical fallback: extract exception events first; resort to tail-truncated state otherwise.
28	Tier~2 benefit is model-dependent (Qwen +10.9pp; CodeLlama +0.9pp), so trace design should match deployment model capacity.
29	
30	
```

</details>

```text
1	\section{Conclusion}
2	
3	Across four code-specialized models (1.5B--13B), compact exception events (${\geq}$99.33\%) outperform complete traces (77--82\%) by 18--23pp; gradient masking (${\leq}$4\% dilution), cross-dataset transfer (99.4--100\%), and marker-removal robustness show that diagnostic density, not information quantity, drives SFT learning.
4	Practitioners should extract exception events first; tail-truncated traces (${\geq}$512 tokens) recover 93--97\% as fallback.
5	Whether models beyond 13B can overcome signal dilution in complete traces, and whether these findings extend to languages without Python's structured exception hierarchy, remain open questions.
6	
```

```text
1	\section{Related Work}
2	
3	\paragraph{Execution Traces for Code LLMs.}
4	TracePile \citep{chen2025tracepile} scaled trace-augmented training to 2.6M samples; \citet{wang2025dcsh} found trace utility task- and format-dependent, which we extend by showing that complete traces degrade SFT performance while compact exception events suffice.
5	NExT \citep{ni2024next} and ET-CoT \citep{eguchi2025etcot} used trace chain-of-thought for repair and execution reasoning; \citet{ferrando2025execute} found compact traces comparable to full traces, supporting our density finding.
6	CRUXEval \citep{gu2024cruxeval,xu2025cruxevalx} and LiveCodeBench \citep{jain2024livecodebench} established execution reasoning benchmarks including test output prediction, but none decompose which trace components contribute to \emph{classification} accuracy.
7	
8	\paragraph{Code Verdict and Patch Classification.}
9	TRACED \citep{ding2024traced} showed execution-aware pretraining improves vulnerability detection; CC2Vec \citep{hoang2020cc2vec}, LLM4PatchCorrect \citep{zhou2024llm4patch}, and APPT \citep{appt2024} developed representation learning of code changes.
10	Verdict prediction is an instance of the test oracle problem \citep{barr2015oracle}.
11	
12	\paragraph{Fault Localization and Trace Selection.}
13	SBFL \citep{abreu2007accuracy,jones2002visualization,wong2014dstar} and LLM-based FL \citep{kang2024autofl,qin2024agentfl} share our insight that anomalous execution events carry the strongest diagnostic signal.
14	TraceCoder \citep{huang2026tracecoder} and InspectCoder \citep{wang2025inspectcoder} use selective trace extraction; agent-based repair systems \citep{bouzenia2025repairagent,xia2024agentless,yang2024sweagent} incorporate execution feedback, yet which trace components carry verdict signals most efficiently remains unstudied.
15	Prompt compression \citep{jiang2023llmlingua,li2023selectivecontext} showed pruning low-information tokens preserves accuracy; our decomposition quantifies \emph{why}.
16	
17	\paragraph{Calibration, Data Selection, and Defect Prediction.}
18	Neural network calibration \citep{guo2017calibration}, extended to LLMs \citep{kadavath2022language,geng2024calibration,spiess2025calibration}, studies confidence-correctness alignment; our ECE ${<}$ 0.005 suggests compact inputs improve calibration.
19	The Information Bottleneck \citep{tishby2000information}, feature selection theory \citep{guyon2003introduction}, and LIMA \citep{zhou2023lima} predict that compressing task-irrelevant information improves learning; data pruning \citep{paul2021deep,sorscher2022beyond} and curriculum learning \citep{bengio2009curriculum} confirm this empirically, consistent with our finding that compact exceptions converge rapidly while verbose traces oscillate.
20	
21	
```

```text
102	%% ============================================================
103	\section{VOTA Extraction Algorithm}
104	\label{sec:vota_algorithm}
105	
106	Algorithm~\ref{alg:vota} describes the three-component VOTA extraction pipeline applied to a raw \texttt{sys.settrace} execution log.
107	
108	\begin{algorithm}[t]
109	\caption{VOTA Trace Extraction}
110	\label{alg:vota}
111	\small
112	\begin{algorithmic}[1]
113	\REQUIRE Raw trace $T$ from \texttt{sys.settrace} (list of events)
114	\ENSURE Compressed VOTA representation $V$
115	\STATE $V \leftarrow$ empty string
116	\STATE \textbf{// Component 1: Exception summary}
117	\FOR{each event $e$ in $T$ with type \texttt{exception}}
118	  \STATE Parse traceback $\rightarrow$ extract error type, message, file, line
119	  \STATE Append \texttt{[ERROR] type: message (file:line)} to $V$
120	\ENDFOR
121	\IF{no exception events found}
122	  \STATE Append \texttt{[No exception data in trace]} to $V$
123	\ENDIF
124	\STATE \textbf{// Component 2: Assertion-point comparison}
125	\FOR{each \texttt{assert} or \texttt{return} event $e$ in $T$}
126	  \STATE Extract actual value from execution state
127	  \STATE Extract expected value from test assertion
128	  \STATE Append \texttt{[ASSERT] actual=... expected=... match=T/F} to $V$
129	\ENDFOR
130	\STATE \textbf{// Component 3: Loop compression}
131	\FOR{each consecutive repeated line sequence $S$ in $T$}
132	  \IF{$|S| \geq 2$ iterations of same loop body}
133	    \STATE Replace $S$ with single iteration + \texttt{[repeated $\times$N]}
134	  \ENDIF
135	\ENDFOR
136	\STATE Append compressed non-exception lines to $V$
137	\RETURN $V$
138	\end{algorithmic}
139	\end{algorithm}
140	
141	%% ============================================================
142	\section{VOTA Trace Examples}
143	\label{sec:vota_examples}
144	
145	We provide two representative VOTA trace examples illustrating the fail and pass cases.
146	In the fail case, the exception event provides an unambiguous fault signal.
147	In the pass case, the absence of an exception event combined with a matching assertion point confirms correct execution.
148	
149	\paragraph{Fail example.}
150	A \texttt{ZeroDivisionError} triggers an \texttt{[ERROR]} event, providing a direct fault signal that is sufficient for the model to predict \texttt{fail}.
151	
152	\begin{scriptsize}
153	\begin{verbatim}
154	## Function
155	def divide(a, b):
156	    return a / b
157	
158	## Test Input
159	divide(1, 0)
160	
161	## Expected Output
162	inf
163	
164	## VOTA Trace
165	[ERROR] ZeroDivisionError: division by zero
166	
167	## Verdict
168	fail
169	\end{verbatim}
170	\end{scriptsize}
171	
172	\paragraph{Pass example.}
173	No exception is raised, and the assertion point confirms that the actual output matches the expected value.
174	
175	\begin{scriptsize}
176	\begin{verbatim}
177	## Function
178	def add(a, b):
179	    return a + b
180	
181	## Test Input
182	add(2, 3)
183	
184	## Expected Output
185	5
186	
187	## VOTA Trace
188	[LOOP] No loops detected.
189	[ASSERT] actual=5, expected=5
190	
191	## Verdict
192	pass
193	\end{verbatim}
194	\end{scriptsize}
195	
196	These examples illustrate the core asymmetry exploited by the \texttt{exception\_only} condition: fail cases produce an explicit \texttt{[ERROR]} event, while pass cases are characterized by its absence.
197	This binary signal (presence vs.\ absence of an exception) is sufficient for near-perfect verdict prediction.
198

252	%% ============================================================
253	\section{Tokenizer and Truncation Analysis}
254	\label{sec:tokenizer_analysis}
255	
256	DeepSeek VOTA seed 42 (84.45\%) is a training failure driven by tokenizer-induced truncation.
257	Re-evaluation of the seed 42 checkpoint with the standard max\_length=2048 yields 16\% accuracy, consistent with complete model collapse rather than marginal degradation.
258	The previously reported 84.45\% was obtained with max\_length=16384, which partially bypasses tokenizer truncation.
259	Seeds 123 and 456, evaluated under identical conditions, achieve 99.16\% and 99.33\% respectively.
260	The DeepSeek tokenizer averages 2{,}626 tokens per VOTA sample versus 1{,}929 for Qwen, causing 91.7\% of DeepSeek VOTA training samples to exceed the 2{,}048-token context window.
261	The training framework preserves the verdict label but truncates trace content, so the model trains with incomplete diagnostic information for the majority of samples.
262	Seeds 123 and 456 (99.16\% and 99.33\%) demonstrate that DeepSeek can learn VOTA when LoRA initialization avoids the truncation-sensitive basin; the 2-seed mean (99.25\%$\pm$0.12) matches the cross-model VOTA range (98.74--99.54\%).
263	
264	A post-hoc group analysis on seed 42 provides directional evidence: partitioning test samples by whether their training-set counterparts exceeded the context window, intact samples ($N{=}99$) achieve 100\% accuracy while truncated samples ($N{=}1{,}091$) achieve 83.04\% (95\% CI [80.75\%, 85.24\%]; Fisher exact $p{<}.001$), with all 185 errors concentrated in the fail class (fail recall = 31.23\%).
265	Per-class analysis (Table~\ref{tab:per_class}) shows that seed 42 achieves 100\% fail precision but only 45.43\% fail recall, systematically defaulting to \texttt{pass}.
266	
267	Seed robustness is consistent across models: Qwen \texttt{exception\_only} produces identical 99.83\% across all three seeds ($\sigma{=}0$), and DeepSeek achieves 99.39\%$\pm$0.10 (seeds 42/456: 99.33\%, seed 123: 99.50\%).
268	Because \texttt{exception\_only} test inputs contain 48.99\% duplicates (607 unique of 1,190; \S\ref{sec:dataset}), CIs assume $n{=}1{,}190$ independent trials; under deduplication ($n{=}607$), Qwen's accuracy is 99.67\% (Wilson CI [98.81, 99.91]).
269	
270	%% ============================================================
271	\section{Persistent Error Analysis}
272	\label{sec:irreducible}
273	
274	The \texttt{exception\_only} condition with Qwen-2.5-Coder-7B misclassifies exactly 2 out of 1,190 test samples across all three seeds (42, 123, 456), with 100\% overlap across seeds.
275	Both samples (IDs 901 and 905) share the following characteristics:
276	
277	\begin{itemize}
278	\item \textbf{Function:} \texttt{check\_dict\_case} (HumanEval/95), a function that checks whether all dictionary keys are uniformly upper-case or lower-case strings.
279	\item \textbf{Ground-truth label:} \texttt{pass}.
280	\item \textbf{Predicted label:} \texttt{fail} (false negative).
281	\item \textbf{Cross-seed consistency:} Both samples are misclassified identically by all three seeds, indicating a systematic rather than stochastic error.
282	\end{itemize}
283	
284	Manual inspection reveals the root cause: \texttt{check\_dict\_case} uses \texttt{all()} over a generator expression, and Python's \texttt{all()} short-circuits on the first \texttt{True} value, raising \texttt{GeneratorExit} to terminate the generator.
285	Because \texttt{GeneratorExit} is a \texttt{BaseException} subclass, the tracer records it as seven \texttt{[ERROR] GeneratorExit} lines, even though the function executes correctly and the test passes.
286	The model observes these spurious error markers and reasonably but incorrectly predicts \texttt{fail}.
287	This is not a model failure but a representation ambiguity: the \texttt{exception\_only} format cannot distinguish genuine exceptions from benign \texttt{BaseException} subclasses triggered by Python's iterator protocol.
288	
289	Most configurations converge to these same 2 errors: Qwen-1.5B, Qwen-7B (3 seeds), CodeLlama-13B (seeds 42/456), and DeepSeek-6.7B (seeds 42/456) all produce error set $\{901, 905\}$.
290	However, CodeLlama-13B seed 123 achieves 100\% accuracy (0/1190 errors), correctly classifying both samples.
291	This demonstrates that 99.83\% is a near-perfect \emph{floor} (the convergence attractor for most LoRA initializations) rather than a hard data ceiling: the representation supports perfect accuracy, but most initializations converge to the same 2-error basin.
292	The practical implication is that these errors are high-probability convergence artifacts addressable through seed selection or ensemble methods, not fundamental limitations of the exception-based representation.
293	
294	%% ============================================================
295	\section{Exception Type Accuracy Breakdown}
296	\label{sec:exception_type}
297	
298	Table~\ref{tab:exception_type} reports \texttt{exception\_only} accuracy stratified by exception type on Qwen-2.5-7B (seed=42, $N{=}1{,}190$).
299	All standard exception types (\texttt{TypeError}, \texttt{ValueError}, \texttt{IndexError}, etc.) achieve 100\% accuracy.
300	The only misclassifications arise from \texttt{GeneratorExit}, a \texttt{BaseException} subclass that is not an \texttt{Exception}. It is raised by Python's iterator protocol during generator short-circuiting, not by actual program errors.

331	\label{sec:exc_subcomponent}
332	
333	To test whether verdict prediction depends on a specific exception subcomponent or merely the binary presence of an exception event, we train Qwen-2.5-7B on three subcomponent-only conditions (seed 42, $N{=}1{,}190$).

352	
353	All three subcomponents independently achieve ${\geq}$99.50\% with 100\% fail recall (Table~\ref{tab:exc_subcomponent}), showing that no single subcomponent is necessary for Tier~1 performance.
354	
355	\paragraph{Format marker confound.}
356	\texttt{exc\_type\_only} and \texttt{exc\_stacktrace\_only} retain the \texttt{[ERROR]} prefix marker present in the full \texttt{exception\_only} format; \texttt{exc\_message\_only} contains only the bare exception class name and message text without any format markers.
357	Because the marker alone is sufficient for near-perfect regex accuracy (B5: 99.33\%; \S\ref{sec:nomarker}), \texttt{exc\_type\_only} (100.00\%) and \texttt{exc\_stacktrace\_only} (99.83\%) are confounded with marker-based classification; we therefore do not make hierarchy claims among subcomponents.
358	\texttt{exc\_message\_only} (99.50\%) provides a clean test: without any format markers, it achieves accuracy comparable to the full \texttt{exception\_only} condition (99.83\%), complementing the dedicated marker-removal ablation (\texttt{nomarker}: 97.65\%; \S\ref{sec:nomarker}) as additional evidence that the model learns semantic exception patterns rather than format shortcuts.

429	
430	\paragraph{Non-monotonic pattern (head-truncation).}
431	Under head-truncation (retaining trace prefixes), the trend is non-monotonic: 512 tokens (80.42\%) slightly outperforms 256 tokens (78.82\%), but doubling to 1024 tokens \emph{decreases} accuracy to 77.23\%, a statistically significant 3.19pp drop ($p{=}.002$, 95\% CI [$-$5.29, $-$1.09]).
432	The no-limit condition (81.76\%) recovers and significantly exceeds 1024 tokens ($p{<}.001$), but 1024 tokens is not significantly better than \texttt{no\_trace} (75.13\%; $\Delta{=}{+}2.10$pp, $p{=}.078$, 95\% CI [$-$0.17, $+$4.37]).
433	This non-monotonicity is specific to head-truncation: the 1024-token prefix retains enough irrelevant content to mislead the model while discarding diagnostic events that appear near the trace terminus.
434	Under tail-truncation, accuracy increases monotonically for Qwen-7B and Qwen-1.5B (Table~\ref{tab:tail_cross_model}); only DeepSeek-6.7B shows a mild $-$1.5pp decrease at tail-1024$\to$tail-2048, attributable to architecture-specific long-context sensitivity rather than a systematic mid-length pathology.
435	
436	\paragraph{Comparison to baselines.}
437	All four trace budget conditions significantly exceed \texttt{no\_trace} except 1024 tokens ($p{=}.078$), indicating that raw trace content provides some signal but unreliably so.
438	The gap to \texttt{exception\_only} (99.83\%) is massive for every budget condition (18--23pp, all $p{<}.001$), underscoring that even unlimited raw traces cannot substitute for targeted signal extraction.
439	The best full-trace condition (no\_limit, 81.76\%) still lags \texttt{exception\_only} by 18.07pp (95\% CI [$-$20.34, $-$15.88]).
440	
441	\paragraph{Interpretation.}
442	These results rule out input truncation as the primary explanation for the full-trace performance gap: even with unlimited tokens, the model cannot extract the diagnostic signal that VOTA's targeted compression provides.
443	The non-monotonic dip at 1024 tokens is consistent with a ``worst-of-both-worlds'' regime where the model receives enough noisy context to dilute the signal but not enough to compensate through broader coverage.
444	The result reinforces the information density interpretation (\S\ref{sec:density}): the gap between full traces and VOTA is driven by the \emph{content} of the trace (irrelevant lines diluting the diagnostic signal), not its \emph{length}.
445	In contrast, VOTA's compressed representation concentrates the diagnostic signal into fewer tokens, making it sensitive to truncation when those tokens are removed (\S\ref{sec:cross_model}).
446	
447	\paragraph{Truncation direction.}
448	Tail-truncation (retaining the last $k$ tokens) dramatically outperforms head-truncation: \texttt{tail\_2048} achieves 97.31\% vs.\ \texttt{head\_2048} (no\_limit) at 81.76\%, a 15.55pp improvement.
449	Even \texttt{tail\_512} (93.61\%) exceeds every head-truncation condition by over 11pp.
450	
451	However, \texttt{tail\_256} (77.48\%) performs at the \texttt{no\_trace} baseline level (75.13\%), indicating that the diagnostic signal does not reside in the very last trace lines.
452	The critical jump occurs between 256 and 512 tail tokens (+16.13pp), localizing the signal window to approximately 256--512 tokens from the trace terminus.
453	This is consistent with the trace format: the globals module dump consumes the first ${\sim}$384 tail tokens (pure noise), so structured execution markers (\texttt{[Line~X]} variable values) only become visible when the budget exceeds this threshold.
454	
455	The 18.07pp gap from \texttt{full\_trace} to \texttt{exception\_only} decomposes as: 86\% positional concentration (recovered by tail-truncation at 2048 tokens) and 14\% residual content noise (the 2.52pp gap from \texttt{tail\_2048} to \texttt{exception\_only} that persists regardless of truncation direction).
456	The fact that \texttt{exception\_only} (99.83\%) still outperforms \texttt{tail\_2048} (97.31\%) indicates that semantic extraction (identifying the exception type and message) is superior to positional extraction (keeping a fixed window), because the model need not filter out non-diagnostic lines that happen to fall within the window.
457	
458	\paragraph{Cross-model budget curve.}
459	Table~\ref{tab:tail_cross_model} extends the tail-truncation analysis to a full budget sweep across four models spanning three architecture families and an 8.7$\times$ parameter range.
460

CodeLlama tail entries are not directly comparable to other models (which use full-sequence loss). $^\ddag$CodeLlama tail-2048 outlier (41.8\%): LoRA long-sequence instability under label-only loss; excluded from cross-model analysis.}

498	
499	Four patterns emerge from the budget curve.
500	\emph{Phase transition between 384 and 512 tokens}: the tail-384 column reveals that the transition is not instantaneous at 256$\to$512 but localized between 384 and 512 tokens.
501	At tail-384, all four models remain near baseline (73.6--80.7\%), gaining only +1.0--4.8pp over tail-256.
502	The steep jump occurs at 384$\to$512: +13.7pp (Qwen-7B), +13.2pp (DeepSeek), +15.5pp (CodeLlama), +22.0pp (Qwen-1.5B), crossing from near-baseline to near-ceiling.
503	This localizes the critical threshold to ${\sim}$400--500 tokens: below ${\sim}$384 tokens, the entire token budget is consumed by globals module dumps (pure noise); above ${\sim}$512, at least one \texttt{[Line~X]} execution event marker becomes visible, regardless of model capacity or architecture.
504	
505	\emph{Convergence at tail-512+}: once the sharp performance jump is crossed, accuracy converges across all models into a 93.6--98.2\% band at tail-512 (seed 42).
506	Multi-seed validation shows robustness: across all 12 model$\times$seed combinations at tail-512 (Table~\ref{tab:tail512_seeds}), all accuracies fall within [93.61\%, 98.24\%], a 4.63pp band that compresses the signal-poor tail-256 spread (6.3pp) by 27\%.
507	CodeLlama-13B shows perfect per-model convergence at tail-512: all three seeds achieve 98.24\% ($\sigma{=}$0), consistent with the 13B scale and label-only loss providing a stronger optimization signal in the signal-rich regime (Table~\ref{tab:tail512_seeds}).
508	However, CodeLlama's higher tail-512 accuracy may partly reflect label-only loss rather than model capacity; direct comparison with other models (which use full-sequence loss) is confounded by this training difference.
509	
510	\begin{table}[t]
511	\centering
512	\caption{Cross-seed convergence at the tail-512 truncation point ($N{=}1{,}190$ per seed). All 12 model$\times$seed combinations fall within a 4.63pp band [93.61\%, 98.24\%]. CodeLlama-13B achieves identical accuracy across all three seeds ($\sigma{=}$0).}
513	\label{tab:tail512_seeds}
514	\small
515	\resizebox{\columnwidth}{!}{%
516	\begin{tabular}{lccccr}
517	\toprule
518	\textbf{Model} & \textbf{S42} & \textbf{S123} & \textbf{S456} & \textbf{Mean} & \textbf{$\sigma$} \\
519	\midrule
520	Qwen-7B (7B)      & 93.61 & 96.00 & 97.65 & 95.75 & 2.05 \\
521	DeepSeek (6.7B)    & 93.95 & 95.04 & 96.10 & 95.03 & 1.08 \\
522	Qwen-1.5B (1.5B)  & 95.63 & 97.65 & 96.72 & 96.67 & 1.01 \\
523	CodeLlama (13B)    & 98.24 & 98.24 & 98.24 & \textbf{98.24} & \textbf{0.00} \\
524	\midrule
525	\multicolumn{4}{l}{Cross-model band} & \multicolumn{2}{r}{[93.61, 98.24] = 4.63pp} \\
526	\bottomrule
527	\end{tabular}%
528	}
529	\end{table}
530	
531	At tail-1024, four models reach 96.5--98.2\% (CodeLlama 98.2\% under label-only loss, three others 96.5--96.6\%).
532	Scale and architecture differences that dominate the signal-poor regime (tail-256 range: 72.6--78.9\%, 6.3pp) compress dramatically in the signal-rich regime.
533	
534	\emph{Signal-poor convergence band}: head-2048 accuracies across all four models also converge into a narrow 75.3--81.8\% band (6.5pp; Table~\ref{tab:head_vs_tail}), comparable in width to the tail-256 band (72.6--78.9\%, 6.3pp).
535	When the diagnostic signal is absent (head-truncation discards trace terminus) or unreachable (tail-256 is too short), models of different scale and architecture collapse to similar performance, suggesting a shared floor determined by code comprehension rather than trace processing.
536	
537	\emph{Scale behavior inversion}: in the signal-poor regime (tail-256), smaller models perform worse (Qwen-1.5B 72.6\% vs.\ Qwen-7B 78.9\%, $-$6.3pp), mirroring the \texttt{no\_trace} scale pattern.
538	In the signal-rich regime (tail-512+), this reverses: Qwen-1.5B matches or exceeds Qwen-7B (95.6\% vs.\ 93.6\% at tail-512; 97.9\% vs.\ 97.3\% at tail-2048), consistent with sufficient trace signal eliminating the capacity advantage.
539	
540	\emph{Non-monotonicity at long sequences}: two models show accuracy decreases beyond tail-1024.
541	DeepSeek-6.7B drops $-$1.5pp from tail-1024 (96.5\%) to tail-2048 (95.0\%), consistent with its known long-context sensitivity (\S\ref{sec:training_dynamics}).
542	CodeLlama-13B exhibits a more severe collapse: tail-2048 under label-only loss drops to 41.8\% (vs.\ tail-1024 = 98.2\%), which we attribute to LoRA long-sequence instability under the label-only loss regime; this outlier is excluded from cross-model tail-2048 analysis.
543	Qwen-7B and Qwen-1.5B increase monotonically across all budgets.
544	
545	Table~\ref{tab:head_vs_tail} shows the truncation direction effect: among the three models with complete data, tail-2048 outperforms head-2048 by 15.5--22.6pp.
546	The gap is largest for Qwen-1.5B (+22.6pp), indicating that smaller models benefit most from positional proximity to the diagnostic signal, as they lack the capacity to locate it within longer, noisier prefixes.
547	
548	%% ============================================================
549	\section{Calibration Analysis}
550	\label{sec:calibration_full}
551	
552	Beyond accuracy, we evaluate whether trace conditions affect prediction calibration, motivated by recent findings that code LLM confidence is often poorly calibrated \citep{spiess2025calibration}.
553	
554	\begin{table}[t]
555	\centering
556	\caption{Calibration metrics on Qwen-2.5-7B ($N{=}1{,}190$, seed=42). ECE = expected calibration error; Brier = Brier score. Lower is better for both.}
557	\label{tab:calibration}
558	\small
559	\begin{tabular}{lcc}
560	\toprule
561	\textbf{Condition} & \textbf{ECE $\downarrow$} & \textbf{Brier $\downarrow$} \\
562	\midrule
563	ae\_only & \textbf{0.0004} & \textbf{0.0008} \\
564	exception\_only & 0.0027 & 0.0013 \\
565	VOTA & 0.0054 & 0.005 \\
566	full\_trace & 0.096 & 0.144 \\
567	no\_trace & 0.171 & 0.197 \\
568	\bottomrule
569	\end{tabular}
570	\end{table}
571	
572	Table~\ref{tab:calibration} shows that calibration quality mirrors the Tier~1/2 hierarchy.
573	Tier~1 conditions (\texttt{ae\_only}: ECE=0.0004; \texttt{exception\_only}: ECE=0.0027) produce near-perfectly calibrated predictions, while \texttt{full\_trace} (ECE=0.096) and \texttt{no\_trace} (ECE=0.171) exhibit substantial miscalibration.
574	VOTA (ECE=0.0054) is well-calibrated but slightly worse than either Tier~1 component alone, reinforcing the pattern that combining trace signals dilutes rather than improves the prediction signal.
575	
576	The Brier scores tell a consistent story: \texttt{ae\_only} (0.0008) and \texttt{exception\_only} (0.0013) achieve near-zero scores, while \texttt{no\_trace} (0.197) indicates substantial miscalibration.
577	
578	\paragraph{Cross-model calibration.}
579	Table~\ref{tab:calibration_cross} extends the calibration analysis to four models (1.5B--13B) under \texttt{exception\_only}.
580	All four models achieve ECE ${<}$ 0.007 and Brier ${<}$ 0.007, indicating that calibration quality generalizes across architectures and scales.
581	CodeLlama-13B achieves the lowest ECE (0.0020) and MCE (0.0411), while DeepSeek-6.7B shows the highest ECE (0.0064) and Brier (0.0061), consistent with its higher error rate (8 errors vs.\ 2 for Qwen/CodeLlama).
582

586	\label{tab:calibration_cross}
587	\small
588	\begin{tabular}{lccc}

590	\textbf{Model} & \textbf{ECE $\downarrow$} & \textbf{Brier $\downarrow$} & \textbf{MCE $\downarrow$} \\

592	Qwen-1.5B & \textbf{0.0020} & \textbf{0.0013} & --- \\
593	CodeLlama-13B & \textbf{0.0020} & \textbf{0.0013} & \textbf{0.0411} \\

607	%% ============================================================
608	\section{Error Overlap Analysis}
609	\label{sec:error_overlap}
610	
611	We analyze the overlap between error sets of different conditions to determine whether they fail on the same samples (Figure~\ref{fig:error_overlap}).
612	
613	\texttt{exception\_only}'s 2 errors (samples 901 and 905) have zero intersection with the error sets of all other conditions (Jaccard = 0.000 in all pairwise comparisons).
614	This orthogonality arises because \texttt{exception\_only} errors are caused by \texttt{GeneratorExit} false positives (pass samples misclassified as fail due to benign \texttt{BaseException} events), while errors from other conditions are predominantly fail samples misclassified as pass due to insufficient signal.
615	
616	Among non-exception conditions, error sets exhibit hierarchical containment: \texttt{ae\_only} $\subset$ VOTA $\subset$ \texttt{loop\_only} $\subset$ \texttt{no\_trace}, with pairwise Jaccard indices increasing with signal overlap.
617	\texttt{loop\_only} and \texttt{full\_trace} share the highest overlap (Jaccard ${\sim}$0.65), reflecting their shared reliance on behavioral patterns rather than explicit fault indicators.
618	
619	This analysis indicates that \texttt{exception\_only} operates on an independent signal dimension: its errors are representation ambiguities (iterator protocol artifacts) rather than information deficits, explaining why combining it with other signals cannot eliminate these specific errors.
620	
621	\begin{figure}[t]
622	\centering
623	\includegraphics[width=\columnwidth]{error_overlap}
624	\caption{Jaccard similarity between error sets of different conditions (Qwen-7B, seed=42, $N{=}1{,}190$). \texttt{exception\_only} (exc\_only) has zero overlap with all other conditions, indicating it operates on an independent signal dimension.}
625	\label{fig:error_overlap}
626	\end{figure}
627	
628	%% ============================================================
629	\section{Cross-Dataset Generalization: DebugBench}
630	\label{sec:debugbench_full}
631	
632	To test whether exception-based verdict prediction generalizes beyond LLM-generated solutions, we evaluate on DebugBench \citep{tian2024debugbench}, a dataset of human-written programs with synthetically injected bugs (single-point mutations such as operator replacement, variable misuse, and missing statements).
633	After filtering for executable Python samples with valid traces, we retain 426 samples with a near-balanced class distribution (majority baseline: 51.4\%).
634	
635	\begin{table}[t]
636	\centering
637	\caption{Generalization to DebugBench ($N{=}426$, Qwen-2.5-7B, seed=42). Wilson 95\% CIs. The component ranking (exception\_only $\geq$ VOTA $>$ no\_trace) is preserved, and exception\_only achieves perfect accuracy.}
638	\label{tab:debugbench}
639	\small
640	\begin{tabular}{lcc}
641	\toprule
642	\textbf{Condition} & \textbf{Acc (\%)} & \textbf{95\% CI} \\
643	\midrule
644	exception\_only & \textbf{100.00} & [99.14, 100.00] \\
645	VOTA & 99.77 & [99.30, 100.00] \\
646	no\_trace & 96.71 & [94.80, 98.40] \\
647	\midrule
648	Majority baseline & 51.41 & --- \\
649	\bottomrule
650	\end{tabular}
651	\end{table}
652	
653	Table~\ref{tab:debugbench} shows that the component ranking from the main experiments is preserved: \texttt{exception\_only} (100.00\%) $\geq$ VOTA (99.77\%) $>$ \texttt{no\_trace} (96.71\%).
654	The \texttt{exception\_only} condition achieves perfect accuracy, showing that the exception-based representation generalizes to a different fault distribution without degradation.
655	
656	The absolute margins are much narrower than on the main dataset: the \texttt{no\_trace} baseline already achieves 96.71\% (compared to 75.13\% on HumanEval+MBPP), leaving only 3.29pp of headroom for trace-based improvement.
657	This ceiling effect reflects a fundamental difference in fault characteristics: DebugBench bugs are synthetically injected single-point mutations, which produce code that is structurally dissimilar from the original correct solution; a model can often identify the fault from source code alone.
658	In contrast, the HumanEval+MBPP solutions are LLM-generated, producing natural errors (logic mistakes, edge-case failures) where the faulty and correct solutions may be structurally similar, making code-only verdict prediction substantially harder.
659	
660	These results support exception-based verdict prediction as a generalizable approach: the method does not degrade on an out-of-distribution dataset, and the exception dominance ranking (exception\_only $\geq$ VOTA $>$ no\_trace) is consistent across both datasets. However, the narrow DebugBench margins are insufficient to independently validate trace importance.
661	The 24.70pp trace lift on the main dataset (no\_trace 75.13\% $\to$ exception\_only 99.83\%) remains the primary evidence for the value of exception-based trace signals.
662	
663	%% ============================================================
664	\section{Cross-Model Robustness Analysis}
665	\label{sec:robustness_analysis}
666	
667	The cross-model gap pattern (Table~\ref{tab:cross_model}) suggests a design principle: \emph{trace representations should match the model capability floor, not the ceiling.}
668	Exception events are consistently the most effective component on both models, and their structural simplicity (a fixed-format error message) ensures that the relative advantage over richer representations holds across all seeds.
669	All three DeepSeek \texttt{exception\_only} seeds achieve ${\geq}$99.33\% (99.33/99.50/99.33, $\sigma{=}$0.10pp), matching Qwen and CodeLlama at ${\geq}$99.83\%.
670	VOTA achieves ${\geq}$98.57\% across all models when excluding outlier seeds (cross-model range: 0.80pp), but DeepSeek VOTA exhibits seed-dependent instability: seed 42 (84.45\%) is a training failure due to tokenizer-induced truncation (91.7\% of training samples affected), while seeds 123/456 (99.16\%/99.33\%) match cross-model performance.
671	This VOTA instability is confounded by tokenizer efficiency and base model differences \citep{yang2024generalization}.
672	Our \texttt{exception\_only} design sidesteps the truncation confound entirely: inputs have a median of 150 tokens (mean 398), well below the 2{,}048-token context window, so no training samples are truncated on either model.
673	The resulting 0.50pp cross-model gap (Table~\ref{tab:cross_model}) is therefore a clean measure of model capability differences, making the ``capability floor'' principle directly testable.
674	The practical implication is that trace-based systems should default to the simplest sufficient signal.
675	
676	%% ============================================================
677	\section{Training Dynamics and Loss Decoupling}
678	\label{sec:training_dynamics}
679	
680	\begin{figure}[t]
681	\centering
682	\includegraphics[width=\columnwidth]{training_dynamics}
683	\caption{Training dynamics: \texttt{exception\_only} converges to 99.83\% by step 150; \texttt{full\_trace} with label-only loss oscillates (82--89\%) without convergence.}
684	\label{fig:training_dynamics}
685	\end{figure}
686	
687	The DeepSeek \texttt{exception\_only} results are consistent across all three seeds (99.33/99.50/99.33, $\sigma{=}$0.10pp), matching Qwen's cross-seed stability.
688	Epoch-level analysis shows mild degradation in seed 42: accuracy peaks at 99.83\% after epoch~1 but settles at 99.33\% by epoch~3 ($-$0.50pp), while seed 456 shows no degradation (99.33\% at both epochs).
689	Early stopping may yield marginal gains, but the epoch effect is small (${\leq}$0.5pp).
690	The practical recommendation: for deployment, use task-specific metrics (accuracy, fail recall) for checkpoint and seed selection rather than training loss, and train multiple seeds to select the best.
691	
692	The contrast with VOTA is instructive: DeepSeek VOTA seed 42 accuracy monotonically increases from 78.15\% to 84.45\% across epochs, consistent with a performance limit from high trace truncation (91.7\% of samples; \S\ref{sec:tokenizer_analysis}).
693	Seeds 123/456, which avoid the truncation-sensitive initialization basin, achieve 99.16\%/99.33\%, indicating that the deficit is seed-specific rather than a fundamental model limitation.
694	
695	%% ============================================================
696	\section{Deduplication Statistics}
697	\label{sec:dedup_stats}
698	
699	Table~\ref{tab:dedup} reports the duplication rates across trace formats.
700	Because multiple passing solutions to the same problem produce identical (input, output) pairs in the \texttt{exception\_only} format (correct executions raise no exceptions, so the trace content is the same regardless of the solution's internal logic), the dataset contains duplicate rows.
701	Train--test input overlap is zero (split by problem ID), so no information leakage occurs.
702	The high duplication in \texttt{exception\_only} does not affect our experimental conclusions: after deduplication the training set still contains 2,858 unique samples and the test set 607, providing sufficient coverage for the 591-problem benchmark.
703	
704	\paragraph{Problem-level partitioning and sensitivity analysis.}
705	Data is partitioned at the problem level: 472 training problems and 119 test problems, with zero crossover.
706	Duplicates arise from multiple candidate solutions per problem (up to 10 solutions each), not from cross-problem overlap.
707	To verify that deduplication does not alter any finding, we ran a sensitivity analysis across all 19 experimental conditions (7 trace formats $\times$ 4 models, minus conditions not run): deduplication produces a negligible ($<$0.1pp) accuracy delta in every case.
708	This shows that duplicate samples neither inflate nor deflate accuracy, because duplicates share the same ground-truth label and the model's prediction is deterministic at inference time.
709	Duplicates arise because the \texttt{exception\_only} format collapses passing solutions (which share identical ``no exception'' output) into identical rows; the redundancy is a property of the format's compactness for pass-class samples.
710	The \texttt{exception\_only} test set contains 48.99\% such duplicates; all confidence intervals treat samples as independent Bernoulli trials.
711	
712	\begin{table}[t]
713	\centering
714	\caption{Deduplication statistics. The \texttt{exception\_only} format collapses multiple passing solutions into identical rows because correct executions produce no exception events. Train--test input overlap is 0 (no leakage).}
715	\label{tab:dedup}
716	\small
717	\begin{tabular}{lccc}
718	\toprule
719	\textbf{Split} & \textbf{Total} & \textbf{Unique} & \textbf{Dup.\ rate} \\
720	\midrule
721	exception\_only train & 4{,}720 & 2{,}858 & 39.45\% \\
722	exception\_only test  & 1{,}190 &   607 & 48.99\% \\
723	raw\_traces (full)    & 5{,}910 & 5{,}803 &  1.81\% \\
724	\bottomrule
725	\end{tabular}
726	\end{table}
```
